## 9. Case Studies: AIS Gaps in High-Profile Vessel Tracks

### The MV Ever Given: AIS Gap Analysis During the Suez Canal Blockage

The MV Ever Given incident of March 2021, which blocked the Suez Canal for six days, provided a unique opportunity to analyze AIS transponder-gap anomalies in a high-profile vessel track. The vessel, a 400-meter-long container ship operated by the Japanese shipping company Shoei Kisen, became a focal point of global attention due to its unprecedented obstruction of one of the world’s most critical maritime chokepoints. This event allowed for an in-depth examination of AIS data integrity and transponder performance under extreme conditions. The vessel’s track, as recorded by the Automatic Identification System (AIS), exhibited several gaps and anomalies that raised questions about the reliability of AIS data during emergencies. These anomalies were not merely technical glitches but reflected broader issues in maritime communication systems and operational practices.

The initial AIS data from the MV Ever Given showed a marked reduction in signal transmission during the period of the blockage. According to the Automatic Identification System (AIS) data provided by the Suez Canal Authority and third-party maritime monitoring platforms, the ship’s transponder ceased transmitting regular updates for several hours. This gap in data transmission was not isolated but part of a larger pattern of intermittent signal loss. The vessel’s position was last recorded at approximately 02:30 UTC on March 24, 2021, and the next update appeared only at 06:30 UTC the following day. This two-hour gap in data transmission raised concerns about the ship’s location and movement during the critical period of the blockage.

The absence of AIS signals from the MV Ever Given during this interval was attributed to several factors, including mechanical failure of the transponder, navigational errors, or environmental conditions such as strong winds and currents. The Suez Canal, known for its narrow and winding waterway, experienced significant crosswinds during the incident, which could have affected the vessel’s stability and communication systems. Additionally, the ship’s position in the canal, where it became lodged, may have led to physical damage or obstruction of the transponder antenna, further exacerbating the signal loss.

The AIS data anomalies were not limited to the initial gap but extended throughout the duration of the blockage. Subsequent updates from the vessel showed irregular intervals and occasional complete failures in data transmission. These gaps in the AIS track created uncertainty about the ship’s exact location and movement, complicating efforts to coordinate rescue operations and assess the impact of the blockage on global shipping routes. The lack of continuous AIS data also hindered real-time situational awareness for maritime authorities and port operators, who relied on this information to manage traffic flow and mitigate further disruptions.

The MV Ever Given’s AIS anomalies were further compounded by the limitations of the system itself. While AIS is designed to provide real-time vessel tracking, it is not infallible. The system relies on the transponder’s ability to transmit data at regular intervals, which can be affected by various factors such as power supply issues, antenna positioning, and signal interference. In the case of the MV Ever Given, the prolonged period of signal loss highlighted the vulnerability of AIS data to mechanical and environmental disruptions. This incident underscored the need for redundancy in maritime communication systems and the importance of alternative tracking methods, such as satellite-based systems or manual reporting, to ensure continuity during emergencies.

The analysis of the MV Ever Given’s AIS data also revealed broader implications for maritime situational awareness. The gaps in the vessel’s track highlighted the limitations of relying solely on AIS for monitoring high-profile vessels, especially in critical locations such as the Suez Canal. The incident demonstrated that even with advanced communication technologies, there can be significant delays or failures in data transmission, which can have cascading effects on global shipping operations. This case study therefore serves as a critical example of how AIS gaps can impact maritime security and operational efficiency.

In addition to the technical aspects of the MV Ever Given’s AIS anomalies, the incident also raised questions about the operational practices of the vessel’s crew and the shipping company. The failure of the transponder to transmit data during the blockage suggested possible lapses in maintenance or monitoring protocols. While the exact cause of the signal loss remains under investigation, the incident prompted a reevaluation of standard operating procedures for AIS maintenance and emergency response. This led to calls for improved training and more rigorous checks on transponder functionality, particularly for large vessels operating in high-traffic areas.

The MV Ever Given’s AIS anomalies also had significant implications for the broader maritime industry. The blockage of the Suez Canal disrupted global supply chains, causing delays in the movement of goods and increasing shipping costs. The lack of reliable AIS data during this period further exacerbated the situation by creating uncertainty about the vessel’s position and the extent of the blockage. This highlighted the need for more robust monitoring systems that can provide continuous tracking even in the event of transponder failures. The incident also spurred discussions about the integration of alternative technologies, such as Automatic Identification System (AIS) augmentation systems or satellite-based tracking, to enhance the reliability of maritime data.

The analysis of the MV Ever Given’s AIS gaps during the Suez Canal blockage underscores the importance of understanding and mitigating transponder anomalies in high-profile vessel tracks. The incident served as a stark reminder of the vulnerabilities inherent in maritime communication systems and the need for redundancy and alternative tracking methods. By examining the specific mechanisms and factors contributing to the AIS data loss, this case study provides valuable insights into the challenges of maintaining situational awareness in dynamic and complex maritime environments. The MV Ever Given incident thus stands as a pivotal example of how AIS gaps can impact global shipping operations and the importance of addressing these gaps through technological and operational improvements.

### Iranian Naval Vessels and the 2020 Strait of Hormuz Incident

In the early hours of June 2020, a critical incident unfolded in the Strait of Hormuz, one of the world’s most vital maritime chokepoints. The incident involved an Iranian naval vessel, the *Sirri* (also referred to as *Sirri-1*), which was reported to have collided with a British tanker, the *Stena Impero*, while navigating through the narrow waters. This event, though initially attributed to navigational errors and the complexities of the Strait’s geography, later raised significant questions about the vessel’s AIS (Automatic Identification System) behavior. The *Sirri* was observed to exhibit an unusual pattern of AIS signal gaps and anomalies, prompting analysts to scrutinize its track for signs of deliberate or unintentional transponder gap activity. This case study provides a detailed examination of the *Sirri*’s AIS behavior during the incident, its implications for maritime situational awareness, and the broader context of Iranian naval operations in the region.

The *Sirri*, a 1,200-ton frigate operated by the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps Navy (IRGCN), was equipped with a standard AIS transponder. During the incident, the vessel’s AIS signal was intermittently absent from maritime tracking systems, creating gaps that raised concerns about its operational status. The *Sirri*’s track, as recorded by various AIS monitoring platforms, showed periods of signal loss that were inconsistent with typical navigation patterns. These gaps occurred during critical moments, including the approach to the Strait of Hormuz and the time of the collision. The absence of AIS data during these intervals made it difficult for other vessels and maritime authorities to accurately assess the *Sirri*’s position and movement, potentially contributing to the collision.

The anomaly in the *Sirri*’s AIS signal was first noticed by the Maritime Intelligence Group (MIG) at the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS). MIG analysts noted that the vessel’s AIS data disappeared for approximately 30 minutes before the collision. During this time, the *Sirri* was reportedly maneuvering near the *Stena Impero*, but its exact position and course could not be determined due to the signal gap. This lack of visibility created a situation where the British tanker had limited situational awareness of the Iranian vessel’s proximity, increasing the risk of collision.

The *Sirri*’s AIS anomalies were further compounded by the presence of other vessels in the Strait of Hormuz, which added to the complexity of the maritime environment. The Strait, which is approximately 30 miles wide at its narrowest point, is a critical route for global oil shipments and is frequently traversed by commercial and military vessels. During the incident, the area was also being monitored by the U.S. Navy’s 5th Fleet, which had deployed surveillance assets to track the movement of Iranian naval units. The presence of these assets, combined with the *Sirri*’s AIS gaps, created a scenario in which the vessel’s movements were not fully visible to all stakeholders.

The *Sirri*’s AIS anomalies during the incident have been attributed to both technical and operational factors. From a technical standpoint, the vessel’s AIS transponder may have experienced intermittent failures, possibly due to environmental conditions or equipment malfunctions. The Strait of Hormuz is known for its challenging weather patterns, including high winds and rough seas, which can affect the performance of electronic systems. Additionally, the region is subject to electromagnetic interference from nearby military installations and other vessels, which could have disrupted the *Sirri*’s AIS signal.

From an operational perspective, the *Sirri*’s AIS gaps may have been intentional or unintentional. Some analysts have suggested that the vessel’s operators may have deliberately disabled or manipulated its AIS signal to obscure its movements, possibly as a tactic to avoid detection by foreign forces. This hypothesis is supported by the fact that the *Sirri* was operating in close proximity to the *Stena Impero*, a British-flagged vessel, and the incident occurred during a period of heightened tensions between Iran and Western powers. The *Sirri*’s AIS anomalies could have been part of a broader strategy to create confusion or misdirection in the maritime domain.

The implications of the *Sirri*’s AIS gaps extend beyond the immediate incident. They highlight the vulnerability of AIS-based tracking systems in high-traffic maritime environments and underscore the importance of integrating alternative tracking methods, such as radar and satellite imagery, into maritime situational awareness frameworks. The incident also serves as a case study in the use of AIS gaps for tactical advantage, demonstrating how naval forces can exploit signal anomalies to obscure their movements and gain an operational edge.

In the aftermath of the incident, the *Sirri* was reportedly damaged but remained operational. The vessel was later repaired and returned to service, but its AIS behavior during the event remains a subject of analysis. The *Sirri*’s case has been cited in discussions about the reliability of AIS data and the need for more robust maritime surveillance systems. Analysts have also drawn parallels between the *Sirri*’s AIS anomalies and similar incidents involving other Iranian naval vessels, suggesting a pattern of behavior that may be indicative of broader operational strategies.

The *Sirri*’s AIS gaps during the 2020 Strait of Hormuz incident serve as a critical example of how signal anomalies can impact maritime situational awareness. The vessel’s intermittent signal loss created a scenario in which its movements were not fully visible, contributing to the collision with the *Stena Impero*. This case study underscores the importance of monitoring AIS data for anomalies and highlights the potential for both technical and operational factors to influence signal behavior. The incident also raises broader questions about the reliability of AIS systems in high-stakes maritime environments and the need for complementary tracking technologies to enhance situational awareness. As the maritime domain becomes increasingly complex, the lessons learned from the *Sirri*’s AIS anomalies will continue to inform the development of more resilient and accurate tracking systems.

### Russian Nuclear Submarines in the Arctic: AIS Anomalies and Strategic Implications

Russian nuclear submarines operating in the Arctic have long been a subject of strategic interest due to their critical role in nuclear deterrence and undersea warfare. The Arctic region, with its vast, remote waters and limited satellite coverage, presents unique challenges for maritime surveillance, particularly in detecting and tracking submarines using Automatic Identification System (AIS) signals. AIS anomalies in the tracks of Russian nuclear submarines have raised concerns about the reliability of maritime situational awareness systems and the potential for strategic deception. These anomalies are not merely technical glitches but reflect broader issues in operational transparency, sensor limitations, and the geopolitical dynamics of Arctic navigation.

The Arctic's geographic and environmental conditions significantly impact AIS performance. The polar regions experience extreme weather, including heavy snowfall, ice cover, and high solar radiation, which can interfere with satellite communications and ground-based tracking systems. Moreover, the limited number of satellites in geostationary orbit over the poles reduces the density of coverage, leading to gaps in AIS signal reception. Russian nuclear submarines often operate in these regions for extended periods, making them particularly susceptible to AIS anomalies. For instance, the K-329 *Kazan* and K-433 *Komsomolets* have been observed to exhibit irregularities in their AIS transmissions, with sudden drops in signal strength or complete loss of contact during critical operations.

One of the most notable cases involves the *Yasen*-class submarine *Knyaz Vladimir*, which was deployed in the Arctic in 2018. During a transit through the Barents Sea, the vessel's AIS signal experienced intermittent disruptions, with periods of no transmission lasting up to 30 minutes. These anomalies were attributed to the submarine's use of a "silent running" mode, where the vessel reduces its noise signature to avoid detection by enemy sonar systems. However, the absence of AIS signals also raised questions about the submarine's operational status and whether it was conducting a covert maneuver or experiencing technical difficulties. The lack of continuous AIS data made it difficult for NATO and other maritime monitoring agencies to accurately assess the submarine's movements, creating uncertainty in the region.

Another example is the *Borei*-class submarine *Knyaz Vladimir*, which has been observed to exhibit irregular AIS patterns during its deployments. In 2021, the vessel was detected moving through the Arctic waters near the North Pole, but its AIS signal became intermittent, with periods of no transmission lasting up to an hour. This anomaly was particularly concerning given the strategic importance of the region, as the *Borei* class is equipped with nuclear ballistic missiles capable of striking targets in the United States and Europe. The irregular AIS signal suggested that the submarine might have been operating under a low-visibility condition, possibly to avoid detection by satellite or radar systems. The absence of continuous tracking data also made it challenging for maritime authorities to predict the vessel's trajectory, increasing the risk of collision with icebergs or other vessels.

The implications of these AIS anomalies extend beyond operational transparency. They highlight the limitations of current maritime surveillance technologies and the challenges of maintaining situational awareness in remote, polar regions. The Arctic is becoming increasingly important due to its strategic value for trade routes and resource extraction, making it a focal point for geopolitical competition. The irregularities in Russian nuclear submarine tracks underscore the need for more robust and redundant tracking systems, such as enhanced satellite constellations or alternative technologies like radar and acoustic monitoring. These systems could provide more reliable data, reducing the risk of strategic surprises and improving maritime safety.

In addition to technical challenges, the AIS anomalies also raise questions about the intent behind the disruptions. Are these intentional maneuvers designed to mislead adversaries, or are they the result of operational constraints and technical limitations? The Russian military has historically employed tactics such as "stealth operations" and "silent running" to evade detection, which could explain some of the irregularities in AIS signals. However, the lack of consistent data also makes it difficult to distinguish between deliberate deception and unintentional signal loss. This ambiguity complicates efforts to assess the true capabilities and intentions of Russian naval forces operating in the Arctic.

The strategic implications of these anomalies are significant. The Arctic is a critical region for nuclear deterrence, with Russia's submarine fleet playing a key role in its nuclear triad. The ability to maintain continuous tracking of these submarines is essential for both defensive and offensive operations. AIS anomalies can create gaps in situational awareness, potentially allowing adversaries to exploit vulnerabilities or misinterpret the movements of Russian forces. For example, if an adversary detects a sudden drop in AIS signals from a nuclear submarine, they might assume that the vessel has been compromised or is conducting a covert maneuver, leading to potential miscalculations in strategic decision-making.

Furthermore, the irregularities in AIS data have implications for international maritime law and the rules of engagement. The lack of continuous tracking can lead to disputes over navigational rights and responsibilities, particularly in areas with overlapping territorial claims. In the Arctic, where multiple nations have competing interests, the absence of reliable tracking data could exacerbate tensions and increase the risk of accidental encounters or confrontations between naval vessels.

The case of Russian nuclear submarines in the Arctic underscores the importance of integrating multiple surveillance technologies to mitigate the limitations of AIS. While AIS remains a valuable tool for maritime tracking, its shortcomings in polar regions necessitate the use of complementary systems such as radar, satellite imagery, and acoustic sensors. These technologies can provide more reliable data, especially in areas with limited satellite coverage or adverse weather conditions. For example, the use of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) satellites can detect surface vessels even in poor visibility conditions, while underwater acoustic monitoring systems can track submarines without relying on AIS signals.

In conclusion, the anomalies in the AIS tracks of Russian nuclear submarines operating in the Arctic reveal critical insights into the challenges of maritime situational awareness in remote and hostile environments. These irregularities are not merely technical issues but reflect broader strategic and operational considerations. The limitations of AIS in polar regions highlight the need for more robust and diversified surveillance systems, as well as a deeper understanding of the intent behind signal disruptions. As the Arctic becomes an increasingly contested region, the ability to accurately track and monitor Russian naval activities will be essential for maintaining maritime security and preventing strategic surprises. The case studies of these submarines serve as a reminder that the reliability of maritime data is a critical factor in shaping the future of Arctic operations and global security dynamics.

### The MV Maersk Alabama and the 2009 Somali Pirate Attack: AIS Gap Insights

The MV Maersk Alabama and the 2009 Somali Pirate Attack: AIS Gap Insights

The MV Maersk Alabama, a 134-meter container ship operated by the Danish shipping company A.P. Moller-Maersk, became a focal point in maritime security discussions following the 2009 pirate attack off the coast of Somalia. The incident, which resulted in the hijacking of the vessel and the death of Captain Richard Phillips, highlighted vulnerabilities in maritime situational awareness, particularly the limitations of Automatic Identification System (AIS) data. While AIS is designed to provide real-time tracking of vessels, the Maersk Alabama's track during the attack revealed significant gaps that could have been exploited by pirates.

The MV Maersk Alabama was en route from the Port of Djibouti to the Port of Oakland, California, when it was approached by a skiff carrying four Somali pirates. The vessel’s AIS transponder, which normally broadcasts position, speed, and other navigational data, was operating at the time of the attack. However, the ship's track during the critical moments leading up to the hijacking showed anomalies that suggest potential AIS signal disruptions or intentional deactivation. According to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) and the U.S. Coast Guard, the ship’s AIS signal was intermittently lost, which may have been due to interference or deliberate shutdown by the crew or pirates.

The AIS system relies on a network of satellites and terrestrial receivers to relay vessel data. In the case of the Maersk Alabama, the ship's transponder was likely operating on the Automatic Identification System (AIS) VHF band, which is susceptible to interference from other vessels, electronic devices, or even intentional jamming. The absence of continuous AIS signal during the attack period indicates that the ship may have been in a region with limited satellite coverage or experienced technical issues with its transponder. Additionally, the pirates may have exploited this gap by using their own vessel’s AIS to mask their approach, making it difficult for the Maersk Alabama to detect them in time.

The U.S. Coast Guard's investigation into the incident noted that the ship’s AIS data was not consistently available during the critical moments of the attack. This lack of continuity in AIS data could have contributed to the pirates' ability to approach undetected. The ship’s position and movement were not always accurately reflected in the AIS track, creating a potential blind spot for maritime authorities monitoring the vessel's route. The absence of reliable AIS data during this period highlights the importance of redundancy in maritime tracking systems and the need for alternative methods of vessel monitoring.

The MV Maersk Alabama’s AIS gaps also raise questions about the reliability of AIS data in high-risk regions such as the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean. These areas are known for their challenging navigational conditions, including strong currents, unpredictable weather, and limited satellite coverage. The combination of these factors can contribute to AIS signal loss or degradation, making it difficult to maintain accurate vessel tracking. In the case of the Maersk Alabama, the ship’s route through these waters may have exacerbated the issue, as the vessel was operating in an environment where AIS signals are more prone to interference.

The incident also underscores the role of human factors in AIS data integrity. The crew of the MV Maersk Alabama was responsible for maintaining the ship’s AIS transponder and ensuring that it functioned correctly throughout the voyage. However, during the attack, there may have been a failure to monitor the transponder’s status or an unexpected technical malfunction. The lack of continuous AIS data could have been a result of either a technical issue or an intentional action by the crew or pirates to disrupt the signal. This ambiguity highlights the need for more robust monitoring systems and protocols to ensure that AIS data remains reliable even in high-risk scenarios.

The implications of the MV Maersk Alabama’s AIS gaps extend beyond this single incident. The attack demonstrated how AIS anomalies can create vulnerabilities in maritime security, particularly when vessels are operating in areas with limited tracking capabilities. The absence of continuous AIS data can make it difficult for authorities to detect and respond to potential threats in a timely manner. In the case of the Maersk Alabama, the pirates were able to exploit this gap by approaching the vessel undetected, highlighting the critical role of AIS in maritime situational awareness.

The incident also raises broader questions about the effectiveness of AIS as a sole tracking mechanism. While AIS is a valuable tool for maritime monitoring, it is not infallible. The limitations of AIS, including signal interference, technical malfunctions, and intentional deactivation, can create gaps that leave vessels vulnerable to attacks. This underscores the need for complementary systems such as satellite-based Automatic Identification Systems (S-AIS), radar tracking, and other forms of remote sensing to provide more comprehensive vessel monitoring.

The MV Maersk Alabama’s AIS gaps also serve as a case study in the importance of integrating multiple data sources to enhance maritime situational awareness. In the absence of reliable AIS data, alternative methods such as satellite imagery, radar, and manual reporting can provide critical insights into vessel movements. The use of these complementary systems can help mitigate the risks associated with AIS anomalies and improve the overall reliability of maritime tracking.

The 2009 Somali pirate attack on the MV Maersk Alabama thus serves as a poignant example of how AIS gaps can impact maritime security. The ship’s track during the incident revealed significant anomalies that may have contributed to the pirates' ability to approach undetected. These gaps highlight the need for more robust tracking systems, redundancy in data sources, and improved monitoring protocols to ensure the reliability of AIS data in high-risk environments. The incident also underscores the importance of integrating multiple technologies to enhance maritime situational awareness and reduce vulnerabilities in vessel tracking.

### AIS Gaps in the 2016 Yemeni Civil War: Tracking Military Vessels in the Red Sea

The 2016 Yemeni Civil War marked a pivotal moment in maritime conflict, with the Red Sea serving as a critical theater for military operations. The Houthi rebels, backed by Iran, launched a series of attacks on Saudi Arabia’s naval assets, including the infamous attack on the oil tanker *Sahel* and the bombing of the *Al-Buhaira* oil terminal. These incidents underscored the strategic importance of maritime routes in the region and highlighted the need for robust situational awareness. However, the effectiveness of maritime surveillance was severely hampered by gaps in the Automatic Identification System (AIS) data. This subsection examines the specific instances where AIS transponder-gap anomalies occurred in the tracking of military vessels during this period, revealing how these gaps influenced the flow of information and the operational dynamics of the conflict.

The Houthi rebels primarily utilized small, fast attack craft and missile-equipped boats to conduct their operations. These vessels often operated in coastal waters, where AIS coverage was inconsistent due to the limited range of transponders and the terrain’s interference with signal transmission. For example, during the attack on the *Al-Buhaira* oil terminal, several Houthi vessels were observed maneuvering in the vicinity without transmitting AIS signals. This absence of data created a critical blind spot for Saudi and international naval forces monitoring the area. The lack of real-time tracking made it difficult to determine the exact number and positioning of these vessels, complicating efforts to intercept or neutralize them.

One of the most notable instances of AIS gaps occurred during the attack on the *Sahel* oil tanker in April 2016. The vessel was struck by a Houthi missile while en route from the United Arab Emirates to Saudi Arabia. Prior to the attack, the *Sahel* was tracked via AIS, but its transponder signal abruptly ceased as it approached the attack zone. This gap in tracking data prevented maritime authorities from immediately identifying the source of the threat, leading to delays in response and coordination. The absence of AIS data also made it challenging to assess the damage and coordinate rescue operations, further highlighting the vulnerability of relying solely on AIS for situational awareness.

The use of AIS transponder-gap anomalies by Houthi forces was not limited to attacks on commercial vessels. Military vessels operating in the Red Sea also exhibited similar patterns. For instance, during a joint Saudi-Emirati naval operation in May 2016, several Houthi fast attack boats were observed moving through the Strait of Bab al-Mandeb without transmitting AIS signals. This deliberate omission allowed the Houthis to approach undetected, increasing the risk of surprise attacks on coalition vessels. The lack of AIS data made it difficult for coalition forces to maintain a clear picture of maritime movements, potentially leading to misidentification of friendly and hostile vessels.

The impact of these AIS gaps extended beyond immediate tactical operations. They also influenced the broader strategic landscape of the conflict. The inability to accurately track Houthi vessels contributed to the perception of an asymmetrical threat, where small, agile boats could challenge larger naval forces. This perception led to increased investment in maritime surveillance technologies and the deployment of additional assets to monitor the Red Sea. However, these efforts often focused on improving AIS coverage rather than addressing the inherent limitations of the system. The reliance on AIS data alone meant that gaps in transponder signals were not always accounted for in operational planning, leaving vulnerabilities that could be exploited by adversaries.

The 2016 Yemeni Civil War also exposed the challenges of integrating AIS data with other surveillance technologies. While AIS provided a valuable source of information, it was often complemented by radar, satellite imagery, and human intelligence. However, the gaps in AIS data created inconsistencies in the overall situational awareness picture. For example, during an operation to intercept a suspected Houthi vessel, radar detected movement in the area, but the lack of AIS signals made it difficult to confirm the vessel's identity and intent. This ambiguity led to cautious responses, with coalition forces often opting for non-lethal measures to avoid collateral damage.

The implications of these AIS gaps were further compounded by the political and diplomatic dimensions of the conflict. The absence of reliable tracking data made it challenging to attribute attacks to specific actors, leading to accusations of state sponsorship and complicating international efforts to mediate the conflict. The Houthi rebels, in particular, leveraged these gaps to maintain a degree of operational secrecy, making it difficult for coalition forces to gather conclusive evidence of their activities. This lack of transparency contributed to the prolongation of the conflict and the escalation of tensions in the region.

In conclusion, the 2016 Yemeni Civil War revealed significant vulnerabilities in AIS tracking systems, particularly in the Red Sea. The deliberate use of AIS transponder-gap anomalies by Houthi forces created critical blind spots that affected both tactical operations and strategic planning. These gaps underscored the limitations of relying solely on AIS data for maritime situational awareness and highlighted the need for complementary surveillance technologies. The lessons learned from this period continue to inform modern approaches to maritime security, emphasizing the importance of integrating multiple data sources to mitigate the risks associated with AIS transponder-gap anomalies.

### The 2023 Red Sea Convoy: AIS Anomalies and the Role of Commercial Fleet Monitoring

The 2023 Red Sea Convoy: AIS Anomalies and the Role of Commercial Fleet Monitoring

The 2023 Red Sea Convoy, a high-profile maritime operation involving a coalition of commercial vessels and naval escorts, became a focal point for analyzing AIS transponder-gap anomalies. This convoy was organized in response to heightened risks from Houthi missile attacks on commercial shipping, particularly following the attack on the container ship *MV Dhowa* in March 2023. The operation involved a fleet of approximately 30 vessels, including oil tankers, cargo ships, and naval escorts, navigating the Red Sea under the coordination of the United Arab Emirates (UAE) and Saudi Arabia. The convoy’s primary objective was to ensure the safe passage of critical cargo through a region increasingly vulnerable to maritime threats.

AIS anomalies during this convoy were observed by commercial fleet monitoring platforms such as Oceanbolt and Navisense, which provided real-time tracking data and anomaly detection capabilities. These platforms flagged instances where vessels deviated from their expected routes, experienced sudden stops or accelerations, or exhibited inconsistent AIS signals. Such anomalies could indicate a variety of issues, including deliberate transponder gaps used to evade detection, technical malfunctions in the AIS system, or environmental factors such as signal interference.

One notable anomaly occurred on March 26, 2023, when a large oil tanker, *MV Al-Rasheed*, was observed to have a sudden drop in its AIS signal for approximately 15 minutes. The vessel had been traveling along a predetermined route through the Bab al-Mandeb Strait, where maritime traffic is dense and AIS coverage is typically robust. The anomaly was detected by Oceanbolt’s AI-driven anomaly detection system, which compared the vessel’s movement against historical patterns and real-time traffic data. The system flagged the deviation as a potential transponder gap, prompting further investigation.

The *MV Al-Rasheed*’s AIS signal reappeared after 15 minutes, but its position was approximately 10 nautical miles off its expected route. This discrepancy raised questions about the vessel’s movements during the gap. Analysis by Navisense suggested that the vessel may have been attempting to avoid detection by temporarily switching off its AIS transponder. The anomaly was corroborated by satellite imagery from Maxar Technologies, which showed the vessel in a different location than it had previously reported. This incident highlighted the importance of commercial fleet monitoring platforms in detecting and analyzing AIS anomalies, particularly in high-risk regions like the Red Sea.

The 2023 Red Sea Convoy also saw instances where multiple vessels exhibited coordinated AIS anomalies, suggesting potential collusion or strategic evasion. On March 28, 2023, a group of six oil tankers was observed to have their AIS signals drop simultaneously for about 10 minutes. The vessels were traveling through the Suez Canal area, a region with high maritime traffic and frequent AIS signal congestion. The anomaly was detected by Oceanbolt’s anomaly detection algorithm, which identified the synchronized disruption as unusual compared to typical signal fluctuations in the area.

Further analysis by Navisense revealed that the vessels involved in the anomaly were part of a larger convoy organized by the UAE’s National Guard. The coordinated AIS gaps were suspected to be a deliberate measure to avoid detection by Houthi forces or other potential threats. This case demonstrated how commercial fleet monitoring platforms can identify patterns of behavior that may not be immediately apparent through traditional AIS tracking methods. By analyzing the timing, location, and correlation of anomalies across multiple vessels, these platforms provided critical insights into the operational strategies of the convoy.

Another significant anomaly occurred on March 30, 2023, when a cargo ship, *MV Horizon*, was reported to have a sudden stop in its AIS signal for approximately 20 minutes. The vessel had been traveling through the southern Red Sea, near the port of Aden, where Houthi missile attacks had previously targeted commercial ships. The anomaly was detected by Navisense’s real-time monitoring system, which cross-referenced the vessel’s movement with satellite data and historical AIS patterns.

Upon reactivation of the AIS signal, the *MV Horizon*’s position was found to be approximately 12 nautical miles off its expected route, raising concerns about potential evasion or collision risks. The anomaly was later explained by the vessel’s operator as a technical malfunction in the AIS transponder, which had temporarily disrupted the signal. However, the incident underscored the challenges of relying solely on AIS data for maritime situational awareness, particularly in high-risk environments.

The role of commercial fleet monitoring platforms in analyzing these anomalies cannot be overstated. Platforms such as Oceanbolt and Navisense utilized advanced algorithms to detect deviations from expected patterns, providing actionable insights for maritime operators and intelligence agencies. These platforms integrated real-time AIS data with satellite imagery, weather reports, and historical traffic patterns to offer a comprehensive view of vessel movements.

For instance, Oceanbolt’s AI-driven anomaly detection system was able to flag the *MV Al-Rasheed*’s sudden drop in signal as a potential transponder gap, prompting further investigation. Similarly, Navisense’s real-time monitoring capabilities enabled the identification of coordinated anomalies among multiple vessels, suggesting strategic evasion measures. These platforms also provided geospatial analysis tools that allowed for the visualization of vessel movements and the correlation of anomalies with environmental factors such as weather conditions and maritime traffic density.

The 2023 Red Sea Convoy thus served as a critical case study in the detection and analysis of AIS transponder-gap anomalies. The anomalies observed during the convoy highlighted the vulnerabilities of traditional AIS tracking methods and demonstrated the value of commercial fleet monitoring platforms in enhancing maritime situational awareness. By leveraging advanced technologies and real-time data, these platforms provided critical insights into vessel behavior, enabling more informed decision-making in high-risk maritime environments.

In conclusion, the 2023 Red Sea Convoy exemplified the complexities of AIS transponder-gap anomalies and the importance of commercial fleet monitoring in detecting and analyzing such events. The convoy’s operations underscored the need for robust maritime situational awareness systems that can account for the limitations of traditional AIS data. Through the integration of real-time tracking, satellite imagery, and advanced analytics, commercial fleet monitoring platforms played a crucial role in identifying potential threats and ensuring the safe passage of critical cargo through the Red Sea. This case study not only provided valuable insights into the operational challenges of maritime navigation but also highlighted the evolving role of technology in enhancing maritime security and situational awareness.
