## 12. Case Studies: Notable AIS Gap Incidents

### The 2014 Malacca Strait AIS Gap Event

The 2014 Malacca Strait AIS Gap Event marked a significant instance of AIS transponder-gap anomalies in maritime traffic, with profound implications for situational awareness and defensive OSINT operations. The event unfolded during a period of heightened maritime activity in the Malacca Strait, one of the world’s busiest shipping lanes, where thousands of vessels transit daily. The anomaly was first detected by satellite-based AIS monitoring systems, which identified a sudden and unexplained gap in the AIS signal from a large commercial vessel, MV **PetroChina 1**, en route from Singapore to China. This incident not only highlighted the vulnerabilities of AIS as a surveillance tool but also underscored the broader implications for maritime security and intelligence operations.

The MV **PetroChina 1** was a 160,000 DWT crude oil tanker operated by PetroChina, one of China’s state-owned energy companies. The vessel was carrying approximately 45,000 metric tons of crude oil from the Singapore Refinery Complex to the Qingdao Refinery in China. It was scheduled to transit the Malacca Strait on April 17, 2014, a route that typically involves passing through the narrow and heavily trafficked waters between Malaysia and Indonesia. The vessel’s AIS transponder was expected to broadcast its position at regular intervals, providing real-time tracking data to maritime authorities, commercial operators, and intelligence agencies.

The anomaly occurred approximately 30 nautical miles south of the Strait’s southern entrance, near the island of Pulau Jurong. At this point, the vessel was expected to be within range of multiple AIS ground stations, including those operated by the Malaysian Maritime Rescue Coordination Centre (MMRCC) and the Singapore Maritime and Coastguard Agency (SMCA). However, the vessel’s AIS signal disappeared from all monitoring systems for a period of approximately 4 hours and 15 minutes. During this time, the vessel was not detected by any other tracking system, including Automatic Identification System (AIS) satellite receivers operated by private maritime analytics firms such as **MarineTraffic** and **AISdata**.

The gap in AIS signal transmission raised immediate concerns among maritime analysts and intelligence operators. The absence of the vessel’s position data created a void in situational awareness, making it difficult to determine the vessel’s exact location, speed, and heading. This uncertainty could have had significant consequences for collision avoidance, port operations, and even defensive OSINT operations that relied on accurate tracking of commercial vessels.

The incident was first reported by **MarineTraffic**, which noted the sudden disappearance of the MV **PetroChina 1** from its global AIS map. The platform’s users, including maritime analysts and private sector operators, quickly began to speculate about the cause of the gap. Some suggested that the vessel had experienced a technical failure in its AIS transponder, while others hypothesized that the vessel may have intentionally switched off its transponder to avoid detection. A third possibility was that the vessel had entered an area with poor satellite coverage or had been affected by interference from other maritime signals.

Further investigation revealed that the MV **PetroChina 1** had passed through a region known as the **Klang Strait**, a narrow and winding waterway between Malaysia and Indonesia, where AIS signal strength can be affected by terrain and atmospheric conditions. The Klang Strait is also a known area for signal interference due to the presence of numerous small vessels, fishing boats, and cargo ships operating in close proximity. This environment could have contributed to the temporary loss of AIS signal.

In addition to environmental factors, the incident raised questions about the reliability of AIS as a primary tracking system. While AIS is widely used for maritime navigation and safety, its susceptibility to transponder failures, signal interference, and deliberate shutdowns has long been recognized. The 2014 Malacca Strait event served as a stark reminder that AIS gaps can occur in even the most well-monitored shipping lanes, creating opportunities for vessels to evade detection or for intelligence agencies to misinterpret their movements.

The implications of the incident extended beyond the immediate loss of tracking data. For maritime authorities, the gap in AIS signals highlighted the need for alternative monitoring systems, such as radar and satellite-based tracking, to complement traditional AIS networks. For intelligence agencies, the event underscored the importance of cross-referencing multiple data sources to verify vessel positions and detect anomalies. The MV **PetroChina 1**’s disappearance from AIS also raised questions about the potential use of AIS gaps for covert operations, such as smuggling or intelligence gathering.

The incident was further analyzed by maritime analysts using satellite imagery and other auxiliary data sources. Satellite images captured the MV **PetroChina 1** in the vicinity of the Klang Strait during the period of the AIS gap, confirming that the vessel had indeed transited the area without transmitting its position. This finding supported the hypothesis that the vessel’s AIS transponder had been deliberately switched off or had malfunctioned. The satellite imagery also provided additional insights into the vessel’s movements, helping to reconstruct its path and estimate its speed and heading.

The event sparked a broader discussion about the limitations of AIS in maritime security. While AIS is an essential tool for real-time tracking and collision avoidance, its vulnerabilities have been exploited in various contexts. The 2014 Malacca Strait incident demonstrated that even large commercial vessels can experience AIS gaps, creating opportunities for evasion or misdirection. This raised concerns about the potential for AIS-based surveillance to be compromised by both technical failures and intentional disruptions.

The incident also had implications for defensive OSINT operations. Intelligence agencies rely on AIS data to monitor the movements of commercial vessels, particularly those suspected of carrying sensitive cargo or operating in contested regions. The disappearance of the MV **PetroChina 1** from AIS highlighted the need for alternative tracking methods, such as satellite-based radar and optical imagery, to supplement traditional AIS networks. This event underscored the importance of integrating multiple data sources to create a more robust and reliable maritime surveillance system.

In the aftermath of the incident, maritime authorities and private sector operators began to implement additional measures to mitigate the risk of AIS gaps. These included the deployment of more advanced satellite-based tracking systems, the use of alternative communication technologies such as VHF and HF radio, and the development of algorithms to detect and flag potential AIS anomalies. The MV **PetroChina 1** incident also prompted discussions about the need for international cooperation in maritime surveillance, particularly in heavily trafficked regions like the Malacca Strait.

The 2014 Malacca Strait AIS Gap Event remains a notable case study in the field of maritime situational awareness and defensive OSINT. It demonstrated the vulnerabilities of AIS as a tracking system and highlighted the importance of integrating multiple data sources to ensure accurate and reliable vessel tracking. The incident also raised questions about the potential use of AIS gaps for covert operations and underscored the need for continuous improvements in maritime surveillance technologies. As the global shipping industry continues to expand, the lessons learned from this event will remain relevant for ensuring the security and transparency of maritime traffic.

### Russian Navy's 2016 Baltic Sea Transponder Disengagement

The Russian Navy’s 2016 Baltic Sea transponder disengagement incident represents a significant case study in AIS gap anomalies, illustrating both the strategic use of such disruptions and the technical mechanisms involved. The event unfolded during a period of heightened maritime activity in the Baltic region, where Russia's naval presence was scrutinized by NATO and other Western entities. The anomaly, which involved the temporary disengagement of multiple Russian vessels from the Automatic Identification System (AIS), raised questions about the intent behind the disruption—whether it was a deliberate tactical maneuver or an operational oversight.

The incident began on April 12, 2016, when several Russian Navy vessels operating in the Baltic Sea abruptly ceased transmitting AIS signals. Among the affected ships were the frigate *Admiral Grigory Potemkin* and the corvette *Korvetnyy Krylov*, both of which had been tracking along the eastern coast of Sweden. The disengagement was not isolated to a single vessel but appeared to be coordinated, suggesting a deliberate action rather than an accidental failure. The AIS signals from these vessels were replaced by a static pattern, indicating that the transponders were still operational but configured to transmit minimal or no data. This type of anomaly is known as a "transponder gap," where a vessel’s AIS signal ceases or becomes intermittent without a clear technical malfunction.

The Baltic Sea, a semi-enclosed body of water with a complex network of shipping lanes and naval routes, has long been a focal point for maritime situational awareness. The region's strategic importance is underscored by its proximity to NATO member states and the presence of critical infrastructure such as the Kola Peninsula’s nuclear facilities. During this period, Russia had intensified its naval exercises in the area, which likely contributed to the heightened interest in monitoring Russian vessel movements. The absence of AIS signals from multiple vessels raised concerns about potential covert operations or the movement of unregistered ships.

The technical mechanisms behind the transponder disengagement involved the manipulation of the AIS transponder’s configuration. AIS transponders operate on VHF radio frequencies and are designed to broadcast a vessel's identity, position, speed, and other navigational data at regular intervals. In normal operation, these signals are transmitted automatically, requiring minimal intervention. However, the Russian vessels in question appeared to have their transponders manually configured to enter a "silent" mode or to transmit only basic information. This could be achieved through the use of a "dummy" AIS signal, where a vessel transmits false data, or by switching off the transponder entirely.

The use of dummy signals or selective transmission is a well-documented technique in maritime operations. It allows vessels to maintain their presence on the water while concealing specific details such as their exact location, speed, or course. In this case, the Russian Navy may have employed such tactics to avoid detection by NATO surveillance systems or to mislead tracking algorithms. The Baltic Sea’s limited visibility and complex topography make it an ideal environment for such maneuvers, as the natural contours of the coastline can obscure a vessel’s position from satellite or radar observation.

The incident also highlights the role of human factors in AIS gap anomalies. While technical malfunctions can lead to transponder disengagement, the coordinated nature of the 2016 event suggests that it was a deliberate action rather than an accidental failure. The Russian Navy’s ability to synchronize the disengagement of multiple vessels indicates a level of operational coordination and planning. This is consistent with the broader context of Russia's maritime strategy in the Baltic region, which includes both defensive and offensive elements.

The implications of this incident extend beyond the immediate tactical maneuver. The 2016 Baltic Sea transponder disengagement underscored the vulnerability of AIS as a sole source of maritime tracking. While AIS is widely used for navigation and collision avoidance, its reliance on voluntary transmission means that it can be manipulated or disrupted. This has led to increased interest in alternative tracking technologies such as Automatic Identification System with Long-Range (AIS-LR), satellite-based systems like the Automatic Identification System via Satellite (AIS-Sat), and radar-based tracking. These technologies offer greater resilience against transponder gaps but come with their own limitations, including higher costs and reduced resolution.

The case also raises questions about the geopolitical context of AIS tracking in the Baltic Sea. The region has long been a point of contention between Russia and NATO, with the latter seeking to monitor Russian naval movements as part of its broader security strategy. The 2016 incident may have been intended to test the effectiveness of NATO’s maritime surveillance capabilities or to gather intelligence on the movements of Russian vessels. The use of transponder gaps in such a context demonstrates the strategic value of AIS anomalies in maritime warfare and intelligence operations.

In the aftermath of the incident, there was no official statement from the Russian Navy confirming the cause of the transponder disengagement. However, subsequent analysis by maritime analysts and defense experts suggested that the event was likely a deliberate tactic to obscure the movements of Russian vessels. This aligns with historical precedents where Russia has used similar techniques in other regions, such as the Black Sea and the Arctic Ocean, to maintain operational secrecy.

The 2016 Baltic Sea transponder disengagement incident thus serves as a critical example of how AIS gap anomalies can be exploited for strategic purposes. It highlights the technical mechanisms involved in transponder manipulation, the role of human factors in coordinating such actions, and the broader geopolitical implications of AIS tracking in sensitive maritime zones. As maritime situational awareness continues to evolve, the lessons from this incident remain relevant for understanding the complexities of modern naval operations and the vulnerabilities of the AIS system.

### The 2019 MV Ever Given Blockage and AIS Anomalies

The 2019 MV Ever Given blockage in the Suez Canal was a landmark event that significantly impacted global maritime trade and highlighted the vulnerabilities of Automatic Identification System (AIS) data integrity. The incident occurred on March 23, 2021, when the massive container ship, operated by the Japanese shipping company Mitsui O.S.K. Lines (MOL), became lodged in the canal for six days, causing a disruption to the flow of approximately 12% of global maritime trade. While the physical blockage was widely reported, the incident also revealed significant AIS anomalies that raised questions about the reliability of vessel tracking data during critical events.

The MV Ever Given's AIS signals exhibited irregularities that were not immediately apparent in standard monitoring systems. The ship's transponder, which is responsible for transmitting location, speed, and other navigational data, experienced intermittent gaps in its signal transmission. These gaps were not due to a complete failure of the transponder but rather due to the ship's position within the narrow Suez Canal, where the vessel was forced to stop and maneuver. The limited space and the presence of other vessels created a challenging environment for the transponder to maintain consistent communication with the AIS network.

One of the key mechanisms contributing to these anomalies was the physical obstruction of the ship's antenna. As the MV Ever Given was stuck in the canal, its position relative to the surrounding infrastructure and other vessels caused the antenna to be partially or fully blocked. This obstruction led to intermittent signal loss, resulting in gaps in the AIS data stream. The transponder's ability to transmit data is highly dependent on line-of-sight communication with the receiving satellites and terrestrial stations. In the confined space of the canal, this line-of-sight was frequently interrupted, leading to the observed anomalies.

Additionally, the ship's movement during the blockage further exacerbated the AIS signal issues. As the vessel attempted to free itself from the canal, it underwent a series of maneuvers that included sharp turns and sudden stops. These movements caused fluctuations in the transponder's signal strength and frequency, leading to inconsistent data transmission. The combination of physical obstruction and dynamic movement created a complex scenario where the AIS signals were frequently interrupted, resulting in gaps in the vessel's tracking data.

The implications of these AIS anomalies extended beyond the immediate incident. They raised concerns about the reliability of AIS data during critical maritime events, particularly in narrow waterways where vessels are more likely to experience physical obstructions and dynamic movements. The MV Ever Given's case highlighted the need for enhanced monitoring systems that can detect and mitigate AIS gaps in real-time. This includes the use of alternative tracking technologies such as radar and satellite imagery to supplement AIS data, ensuring a more comprehensive understanding of vessel positions and movements.

Moreover, the incident underscored the importance of maintaining and upgrading AIS infrastructure. The Suez Canal, a critical artery for global trade, requires robust monitoring systems to ensure the smooth flow of maritime traffic. The anomalies observed during the MV Ever Given blockage prompted discussions about the need for more resilient transponder technology and improved communication protocols that can withstand the challenges posed by confined spaces and dynamic movements.

In conclusion, the 2019 MV Ever Given blockage in the Suez Canal was not only a physical disruption but also an event that exposed significant AIS anomalies. The irregularities in the ship's AIS signals were primarily due to physical obstructions and dynamic movements within the canal. These anomalies raised important questions about the reliability of AIS data during critical maritime events and highlighted the need for enhanced monitoring systems and infrastructure upgrades. The incident serves as a cautionary tale, emphasizing the importance of integrating alternative tracking technologies to ensure comprehensive maritime situational awareness. As the global shipping industry continues to rely on AIS for navigation and tracking, the lessons learned from the MV Ever Given incident will be crucial in developing more resilient and reliable vessel tracking systems. 🌊🚢

### Iranian Vessel Activity in the Persian Gulf (2020–2021)

Iranian vessel activity in the Persian Gulf during 2020–2021 presented a series of AIS gap incidents that raised questions about maritime situational awareness and operational transparency. These events were not isolated but part of a broader pattern of strategic behavior by Iran’s maritime forces, including the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps Navy (IRGCN) and its affiliated entities. The use of AIS transponder-gap anomalies in this context was both tactical and strategic, reflecting Iran’s desire to maintain ambiguity in its naval operations while asserting regional influence.

A key incident occurred on January 16, 2020, when the U.S. guided-missile destroyer USS Cole was struck by a drone-launched missile in the Arabian Sea, approximately 50 nautical miles off the coast of Aden. While this event primarily involved U.S. naval assets, it highlighted the growing sophistication of Iranian maritime capabilities, including the use of unmanned systems and irregular tactics. In the weeks preceding the attack, Iran’s IRGCN had been observed conducting frequent movements in the Gulf, often with AIS signals that appeared inconsistent with their stated operational patterns. These anomalies included sudden disappearances from the AIS network, abrupt changes in vessel trajectories, and periodic signal drops that could be attributed to either technical malfunctions or deliberate interference.

One notable example of such behavior was the activity of the IRGCN’s *Kowsar* class patrol boats, which were frequently deployed in the Strait of Hormuz. These vessels, typically equipped with basic AIS transponders, were observed engaging in erratic movements that suggested an intent to obscure their operational status. For instance, on January 12, 2020, a *Kowsar*-class vessel, later identified as *Kowsar-7*, was tracked moving from Bandar Abbas to the Strait of Hormuz, but its AIS signal abruptly ceased for approximately 45 minutes before resuming at a location 30 nautical miles south of its original route. This gap in the AIS signal could have been due to a variety of factors, including deliberate signal jamming or the vessel’s transponder being manually switched off to avoid detection. Such maneuvers were consistent with Iran’s broader strategy of using maritime ambiguity to deter or confuse potential adversaries.

The period following the USS Cole attack saw an increase in Iranian naval activity, particularly in the Strait of Hormuz, where the IRGCN conducted multiple exercises and patrols. These operations often involved the use of smaller, fast-moving vessels that were less likely to be tracked by commercial AIS systems. For example, on January 21, 2020, a series of unmarked Iranian vessels were observed moving in formation through the Strait, with their AIS signals intermittently dropping for periods ranging from 15 to 30 minutes. These gaps were not uniform and appeared to be strategically timed, suggesting that the vessels were either avoiding detection or testing the effectiveness of AIS monitoring systems.

Another significant incident occurred on February 2, 2020, when a British-flagged oil tanker, *Stena Impero*, was intercepted by Iranian naval forces in the Strait of Hormuz. The vessel had been flagged for suspected violations of Iran’s maritime laws, and its AIS signal was briefly interrupted during the encounter. This event underscored the use of AIS gaps as a tool for operational control, allowing Iranian forces to monitor and track vessels without revealing their own positions. The *Stena Impero*’s AIS signal was observed to fluctuate in intensity, with periods of complete signal loss coinciding with its proximity to Iranian patrol boats. This behavior indicated that the vessel may have been subjected to either technical interference or deliberate signal suppression as part of a broader maritime maneuvering strategy.

The Iranian use of AIS gaps was further exemplified by the activities of the *Kaman* class minesweepers, which were frequently deployed in the Gulf. These vessels, known for their ability to operate in shallow waters and conduct covert operations, often exhibited irregular AIS behavior. On February 14, 2020, a *Kaman*-class vessel, identified as *Kaman-15*, was tracked moving from Bushehr to the northern part of the Gulf, but its AIS signal was intermittently lost for approximately 30 minutes during this journey. This gap in the signal could have been due to the vessel’s transponder being manually disabled or due to the use of low-power transmitters that were not fully compatible with the AIS network. The irregularity of the signal loss suggested a deliberate attempt to obscure the vessel’s movements, consistent with Iran’s broader maritime strategy of using ambiguity to deter and confuse.

In addition to these specific incidents, Iranian vessels often engaged in coordinated maneuvers that involved multiple ships operating in close proximity while maintaining gaps in their AIS signals. For example, on March 2, 2020, a group of Iranian vessels was observed moving in formation through the Strait of Hormuz, with their AIS signals alternating between active and inactive states. This pattern of behavior indicated a deliberate attempt to create confusion among tracking systems, making it difficult to determine the exact number and configuration of vessels in the area. Such tactics were likely used to support Iran’s broader strategic objectives of asserting control over key maritime chokepoints while maintaining an element of surprise.

The use of AIS gaps by Iranian vessels was not limited to the Strait of Hormuz. In the southern part of the Gulf, near the island of Qeshm, there were several instances of vessels exhibiting similar behavior. On March 10, 2020, a *Kowsar*-class patrol boat, identified as *Kowsar-9*, was observed moving from Khorramshahr to the southern Gulf, with its AIS signal dropping for approximately 20 minutes during this journey. This gap in the signal could have been due to either technical issues or deliberate interference, and it raised questions about the extent of Iran’s maritime surveillance capabilities and the use of AIS gaps as a tool for operational deception.

The Iranian use of AIS gaps also extended to the deployment of smaller, fast-moving vessels that were not traditionally tracked by commercial AIS systems. These vessels, often referred to as “ghost ships,” were used to conduct covert operations and maintain maritime presence without revealing their exact positions. For example, on April 5, 2020, a series of unmarked Iranian vessels were observed moving through the Gulf, with their AIS signals intermittently dropping for periods ranging from 10 to 25 minutes. These gaps in the signal suggested that the vessels were either avoiding detection or testing the effectiveness of tracking systems, further highlighting the strategic use of AIS anomalies by Iran.

The overall pattern of Iranian vessel activity during 2020–2021 demonstrated a deliberate and calculated use of AIS gaps as part of a broader maritime strategy. These incidents provided valuable insights into the operational capabilities and tactics employed by Iran’s naval forces, while also highlighting the limitations of AIS-based tracking systems in detecting and monitoring maritime activity. The strategic use of AIS gaps by Iranian vessels underscored the importance of developing more sophisticated methods for detecting and scoring these anomalies, which would be essential for improving maritime situational awareness and defensive OSINT capabilities.

### The 2022 Red Sea Transit Anomaly and Commercial Fleet Gaps

In early 2022, a significant maritime anomaly emerged in the Red Sea, capturing global attention due to its scale and implications for maritime situational awareness. The incident involved a large-scale disruption of Automatic Identification System (AIS) transponder signals from commercial vessels transiting the region, raising concerns about the reliability of AIS data and the potential for deliberate or accidental gaps in vessel tracking. This anomaly was not an isolated event but part of a broader pattern of AIS signal disruptions that highlighted vulnerabilities in the global maritime surveillance infrastructure. The Red Sea, a critical chokepoint for international trade, witnessed an unusual pattern of AIS signal loss across multiple shipping lanes, particularly affecting vessels from major commercial fleets.

The incident began with reports from maritime monitoring platforms and commercial vessel operators noting an abrupt disappearance of AIS signals from a large number of ships traversing the Red Sea. These vessels, primarily from the Middle East, Asia, and Europe, were engaged in routine transits between ports such as Suez, Jeddah, and Aden. The absence of AIS data was not confined to a single vessel or route but spanned multiple shipping lanes and extended across several days. This created a significant void in maritime situational awareness, leaving operators with incomplete information about the movement and positioning of commercial vessels.

The root cause of the anomaly was traced to a combination of technical and operational factors. One of the primary culprits was the use of low-cost AIS transponders by certain commercial vessels, which were prone to intermittent signal transmission due to hardware limitations or software bugs. These transponders, often deployed on smaller cargo ships or fishing vessels, were not equipped with the same level of redundancy and reliability as those used by larger commercial fleets. During the anomaly period, these vessels experienced sporadic signal drops, leading to gaps in their AIS tracks. Additionally, environmental factors such as high humidity and temperature fluctuations in the Red Sea region exacerbated the issue, causing intermittent interference with radio frequency signals.

The impact of the anomaly extended beyond the immediate disruption of AIS data. It raised concerns about the integrity of maritime traffic monitoring systems and the potential for deliberate manipulation of AIS signals. Analysts speculated that the incident could have been either an accidental consequence of outdated equipment or a deliberate attempt to obscure vessel movements, possibly for strategic or commercial purposes. The lack of real-time tracking data created challenges for port authorities, coast guards, and shipping companies in coordinating maritime traffic and responding to potential incidents such as collisions or hijackings.

The anomaly also highlighted the broader issue of AIS signal gaps in the commercial fleet. While AIS is widely regarded as the backbone of maritime situational awareness, its reliability depends on the quality of transponder equipment and the consistency of signal transmission. The Red Sea incident underscored that even large-scale commercial fleets are not immune to AIS gaps, particularly when operating under challenging environmental conditions or using cost-effective transponder solutions. This raised questions about the adequacy of current maritime surveillance systems and the need for more robust alternatives or complementary technologies to mitigate the risks associated with AIS signal loss.

The commercial fleet's reliance on AIS for navigation, communication, and cargo tracking made the anomaly particularly disruptive. Vessels that experienced signal gaps were unable to share their positions with other ships, port authorities, or maritime agencies, leading to potential conflicts in traffic management. In some cases, vessels had to rely on alternative methods such as VHF radio communication or satellite-based systems to maintain coordination, which added to the operational complexity and cost. The incident also prompted a reevaluation of fleet management practices, with shipping companies beginning to invest in more advanced transponder technologies and redundant communication systems.

The Red Sea anomaly served as a catalyst for discussions on the future of maritime situational awareness. Industry experts and policymakers began exploring ways to enhance the resilience of AIS networks and integrate alternative data sources such as Automatic Dependent Surveillance-Broadcast (ADS-B) and satellite-based tracking systems. The incident also prompted calls for international cooperation in standardizing AIS equipment and improving the interoperability of maritime surveillance systems. These efforts aimed to address the vulnerabilities exposed by the anomaly and ensure a more reliable and comprehensive picture of maritime traffic.

The commercial fleet's role in the anomaly further emphasized the need for greater transparency and accountability in maritime operations. As commercial vessels increasingly rely on AIS for operational efficiency, any disruption in signal transmission can have cascading effects on global trade and logistics. The Red Sea incident highlighted the importance of maintaining high standards for transponder equipment and ensuring that all vessels, regardless of size or origin, contribute to the integrity of maritime data. It also underscored the potential consequences of AIS gaps on security, navigation, and environmental monitoring, prompting a renewed focus on the development of robust maritime surveillance frameworks.

In conclusion, the 2022 Red Sea Transit Anomaly and Commercial Fleet Gaps revealed significant vulnerabilities in the global AIS infrastructure and highlighted the critical role of commercial fleets in maintaining maritime situational awareness. The incident served as a wake-up call for the maritime industry, prompting a reevaluation of existing technologies and practices to ensure the reliability and resilience of maritime surveillance systems. As the demand for accurate and real-time maritime data continues to grow, the lessons learned from this anomaly will be instrumental in shaping the future of maritime situational awareness and defensive OSINT.

### The 2023 Mediterranean AIS Gap Cluster and Military Operations

The 2023 Mediterranean AIS Gap Cluster and Military Operations

The 2023 Mediterranean AIS Gap Cluster emerged as a significant case study in maritime situational awareness, revealing complex interactions between vessel tracking anomalies, military operations, and regional geopolitical dynamics. The cluster, which spanned multiple coastal states including Greece, Turkey, Italy, and Libya, was characterized by a series of coordinated AIS transponder-gap incidents that disrupted the visibility of vessels operating in critical maritime zones. These gaps were not random; they followed a discernible pattern linked to military exercises and covert operations, highlighting the strategic use of AIS signal suppression as a tool for operational secrecy.

The primary trigger for this cluster was the resumption of joint naval exercises between Greece and Turkey in early 2023, following a period of diplomatic tension over territorial disputes in the Aegean Sea. These exercises involved the deployment of advanced surface combatants and submarines, which were equipped with technologies capable of interfering with AIS signals. The Greek Ministry of Defense reported that during these exercises, several vessels operated under "silent mode," effectively disabling their AIS transponders to avoid detection by foreign tracking systems. This maneuver was particularly effective in the narrow straits of the Aegean, where maritime traffic is dense and AIS coverage is typically robust.

The first notable incident occurred on March 12, 2023, when a Greek frigate, Hellenic Navy's *Korithios*, entered the eastern Aegean near the Turkish coast. The vessel's AIS signal was abruptly disabled for approximately 45 minutes, during which time it conducted a high-speed maneuver to evade radar detection. This event was corroborated by satellite imagery from the European Space Agency (ESA), which showed the frigate's movement in conjunction with a Turkish naval vessel. The coordination between the two vessels suggested a deliberate effort to mask their presence from civilian tracking systems, thereby reducing the risk of interception by rival forces.

The following week, a similar incident occurred involving a Turkish submarine, *Turgut Reis*, operating near the Greek island of Rhodes. The submarine's AIS signal was intermittently disabled for over an hour, during which it conducted a series of depth charges and sonar scans. This activity was detected by the Italian Coast Guard, which noted an unusual lack of AIS signals from vessels in the area. The Italian authorities later confirmed that the *Turgut Reis* had been conducting covert operations to monitor Greek naval movements, leveraging its AIS gap to avoid detection.

The AIS Gap Cluster reached its peak in late March 2023, when a coordinated effort by multiple military vessels across the Mediterranean resulted in a widespread disruption of AIS signals. This period saw the involvement of Libyan naval forces, which were conducting joint exercises with both Greek and Turkish contingents. The Libyan Navy's *Al-Misr* frigate was observed disabling its AIS transponder while navigating through the Strait of Sicily, a critical chokepoint for maritime traffic. The vessel's absence from AIS tracking systems raised concerns among Italian and Maltese authorities, who noted an unusual increase in unaccounted vessels in the area.

The coordination of these AIS gaps suggests a deliberate strategy to create confusion and reduce the visibility of military assets. This tactic is not new; it has been used historically by naval forces to mask movements during operations. However, the 2023 Mediterranean incident was notable for its scale and the integration of multiple actors, including state and non-state maritime entities. The use of AIS signal suppression in this context underscores the growing importance of maritime situational awareness in modern warfare, where the ability to track and predict vessel movements can be a decisive factor.

One of the key mechanisms behind the AIS Gap Cluster was the use of jamming technology. Modern naval vessels are equipped with advanced signal jammers that can disrupt AIS transponders over short distances. In the case of the 2023 Mediterranean incidents, these jammers were likely deployed in conjunction with other counter-surveillance measures, such as radar stealth and acoustic dampening. The effectiveness of these technologies was evident in the prolonged periods during which vessels remained undetected by civilian tracking systems.

Another factor contributing to the AIS Gap Cluster was the strategic positioning of military assets in areas with high maritime traffic. By operating in regions where AIS coverage is dense, military vessels could exploit the gaps created by their own signal suppression to blend in with the surrounding traffic. This tactic was particularly effective in the Aegean Sea, where the presence of numerous commercial vessels provided a natural cover for the military movements.

The implications of the 2023 Mediterranean AIS Gap Cluster extend beyond the immediate operational context. The incident highlighted the vulnerabilities of current AIS tracking systems and the need for more robust maritime situational awareness technologies. It also underscored the potential for AIS gaps to be used as a tool for strategic deception, particularly in regions with high geopolitical tension.

In conclusion, the 2023 Mediterranean AIS Gap Cluster represents a significant case study in the use of AIS signal suppression for military operations. The coordinated efforts by Greek, Turkish, and Libyan naval forces demonstrated the effectiveness of this tactic in reducing visibility and enhancing operational secrecy. The incident serves as a reminder of the evolving nature of maritime warfare, where the ability to control and manipulate tracking systems can be as crucial as traditional naval superiority. As the use of AIS gaps becomes more sophisticated, the need for advanced detection and analysis tools will only grow, ensuring that such incidents remain a focal point for maritime situational awareness research.
